#133. Streeting denies 'kicking can down the road' in defence spending row

Wes Streeting has defended the government's current approach

Defence Secretary Wes Streeting has denied "kicking the can down the road" over how much the UK spends on defence.

The government has faced criticism for not setting out a pathway at Wednesday's Budget to hit its target of spending 3% of GDP on defence by 2030.

Streeting is at odds with MPs on the Commons defence committee over the issue.

It comes as Chancellor John Healey and Streeting announce that foreign firms which are awarded defence contracts of more than Â£50m will need to invest an equal value back into the UK.

Under the new investment rules, described as an "offset strategy", these companies will need to spend an equivalent amount on British factories, jobs and training.

The Ministry of Defence will apply the rules on a "case-by-case" basis from April 2027, the government said. Healey and Streeting will unveil the policy on a visit to UK defence contractor BAE's shipbuilding site in Glasgow later.

Countries including Poland, Norway and the Netherlands have mandated similar rules to "boost the economic benefit from defence procurement", the government said.

While ministers hope the rules increase investment in Britain's defence industry, they appear designed to do so in a politically palatable way rather than committing to spending cuts.

Tan Dhesi, Labour chair of the cross-party Commons defence committee, said on Wednesday that decisions had been "kicked into the long grass" to next year's government spending review, rather than announced in the Budget.

He warned that without building capabilities now, "we risk leaving ourselves seriously vulnerable in the event of a major conflict".

Streeting hit back at these comments as "unfair" and "unfounded".

Speaking on Thursday, Dhesi said the committee stands by its criticism.

"We had hoped to see that the chancellor would set out a precise pathway to spend 3% of GDP on defence by 2030," Dhesi told BBC Radio 4's Today programme.

"But instead some of those key decisions on defence have lamentably been delayed until next year's spending review."

He praised the government for plugging a Â£4.7bn hole in the Defence Investment Plan in the Budget, through asking government departments to cut their capital budgets by 1%.

Labour MP Tan Dhesi has led the latest criticism of government defence spending

Responding on Thursday, Streeting told Today: "I like Tan very much, he's a friend and I respect him, but I strongly disagree with him.

"And I don't think the charge he makes of delaying or kicking the can down the road stacks up against the extra money that is already going into defence and the capability we're funding right now."

Streeting told BBC Breakfast the new rules, aiming to create "bang for the British buck", would help achieve this target given increasing defence spending "does come at a cost to the country and other things we'd like to spend money on".

He said waiting for the spending review, which does not have a fixed date and could come in the second half of 2027, was "the right thing to do" so ministers can look at "spending in the round".

He added that the UK was ready to deal with hostile threats, but "we're not where we'd want to be".

"There's no sugarcoating the fact that our Armed Forces were under-invested in for more than a decade," he told Breakfast.

Defence analysts have questioned the government's ability to deliver on its 2030 target and the Nato-wide target of 3.5% by 2035. UK defence spending is at an estimated 2.6% of GDP this year.

Some estimates have suggested the government will need to spend an additional Â£10bn annually to reach its targets.

Tory leader Kemi Badenoch said on Wednesday that Healey "failed his own test" by not setting out a "plan for 3%".

Healey resigned as then-Prime Minister Sir Keir Starmer's defence secretary in June over not spending enough on defence.

In the Budget, Healey said the UK was negotiating with Finland, Poland and the Netherlands to set up an international bank - the Multilateral Defence Mechanism - to boost defence investment and joint procurement.

But Conservative shadow defence secretary James Cartlidge said this amounted to "turning to borrowing at a time when the national debt is over Â£3 trillion".

"The answer is not more borrowing â it's to cut welfare to fund defence," he said.

Healey tells departments to make 1% cuts to fund defence

Burnham says he wants to cut welfare bill 'as soon as we possibly can'

#131. Defense, healthcare, crypto and AI: How the midterm election could affect stocks

Lockheed Martin gets about 70% of its sales from the U.S. government. Medicaid accounts for three-quarters of Molina Healthcare's premium revenue. Coinbase is waiting on Congress to finish writing the rules for cryptocurrency.

For some of the biggest U.S. companies, Washington is part of the business model. Now, the midterm election could change who holds the leverage.

A Democratic victory in either or both chambers of Congress would grant the party new leverage to investigate companies, challenge federal spending and demand legislative concessions. But their reach would have limits: President Donald Trump would retain his veto and broad authority over tariffs, immigration and federal regulation.

Historically, stocks have performed well under divided government. Under Republican presidents, the S&P 500 has gained an average of 23% in the year after midterms when Congress was split, compared with 16% when Democrats controlled both chambers, according to a Morgan Stanley analysis published Thursday. Analysts often attribute the pattern in part to congressional gridlock limiting sweeping policy changes.

Still, some analysts caution against assuming that pattern will repeat.

"We don't think that blueprint holds up this time around, and we still see plenty of reasons to be constructive on the U.S. equity market," said Ariana Salvatore, head of U.S. public policy research at Morgan Stanley.

For investors, the midterm election could reshape which businesses preserve their advantages in Washington, particularly in industries such as defense, healthcare, banking and artificial intelligence.

"There are some policy changes that might be market moving, at least for certain segments," said Jeff Buchbinder, chief equity strategist at LPL Financial.

Control of either the House or Senate would give Democrats committee gavels and subpoena power, allowing them to demand documents, compel testimony and haul executives before public hearings.

On Oct. 21, Sen. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., the top Democrat on the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Permanent Subcommittee on Investigations, sought records tied to Trump's White House ballroom from donors including Cisco, Salesforce, ServiceNow and Kraken. Apple and Nvidia each committed $25 million to the project, according to records obtained by The Washington Post.

Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., has separately sought records involving tariff exclusions, including communications involving Apple's former CEO, Tim Cook, as Democrats investigate whether Trump used trade policy to reward political allies and donors.

While donations do not prove wrongdoing or favorable treatment, congressional probes still carry risks: subpoenas consume executive bandwidth, reveal internal communications and generate unpredictable news coverage. Few CEOs relish the idea of sitting at a witness table before lawmakers and television cameras.

"It creates volatility because you get a negative headline," said Monica Guerra, Morgan Stanley's head of policy and geopolitical strategy, who expects Democrats to cast a wide net for their probes.

Democrats have signaled interest in Trump family businesses, government contractors, firms tied to Donald Trump Jr.'s 1789 Capital, and companies associated with Elon Musk and the so-called Department of Government Efficiency.

Then there is the most basic power Congress has: deciding where the money goes.

Trump's 2025 tax cuts and spending law, known as One Big Beautiful Bill, is projected to reduce federal Medicaid spending by $911 billion through 2034, according to a KFF analysis of Congressional Budget Office estimates. KFF is a nonpartisan nonprofit that provides health policy information. Meanwhile, new work requirements for Medicaid enrollees are scheduled to take effect Jan. 1, or earlier in some states, while tighter state budgets could mean fewer people are covered and greater pressure on provider payments.

Molina Healthcare generated $32.2 billion, or 75% of its 2025 premium revenue, from Medicaid, according to the company's annual Securities and Exchange Commission filing. In the same filing, Molina warned that losing government contracts or failing to renew them on favorable terms could materially reduce premium revenue.

A Democratic-controlled chamber could not repeal the 2025 law on its own but could use must-pass budget negotiations to seek funding changes or delays to some cuts.

"They're probably going to push back on those Medicaid cuts," Buchbinder told CNBC, noting that preserving coverage could help stabilize enrollment for Medicaid insurers and patient volumes for hospitals.

Hospitals face their own squeeze: fewer insured patients can mean more uncompensated care, while state budget pressure can reduce reimbursement rates, according to KFF. Congress created a $50 billion rural health fund over five years to cushion some of the blow, but KFF and medical providers have said the money would not fully offset the broader Medicaid reductions.

Democratic gains could still cut both ways for healthcare. The same lawmakers seeking to protect Medicaid funding could also intensify scrutiny of insurers, hospitals and drugmakers over prices, coverage and profits.

Defense contractors face a fight over which weapons and programs Congress keeps funding. The White House has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027, with a focus on replenishing weapons, bolstering readiness and covering other needs, some tied to the Iran war.

The White House has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027, while separately seeking an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, driven primarily by military operations related to Iran.

A Democratic Congress could challenge some Iran- and Israel-related spending while pushing for more aid to Ukraine. However, bipartisan consensus on China deterrence, nuclear modernization and missile defense could protect other major programs, according to multiple analysts.

For contractors, the difference could mean billions of dollars in orders.

Lockheed Martin, for example, recently reached a framework agreement with the Pentagon covering 850 F-35 fighter jets over a decade. But the agreement is not yet a finalized contract: Congress must authorize the multiyear purchase and provide the funding.

"You want to be a stock picker and not just ride the whole sector," Guerra told CNBC, pointing to areas such as shipbuilding, autonomous systems and missile defense as likely winners.

For crypto companies, the midterms could determine whether Trump's regulatory shift becomes durable law or remains vulnerable to the next administration. The industry's push for permanence stalled in September, when the Senate failed to advance the Trump-backed Clarity Act, a sweeping market structure bill.

Shares of Coinbase and stablecoin issuer Circle fell as much as 11% when the measure failed.

Getting a bill through the next Congress could come at a price. Democratic gains would give the party more leverage to demand stricter consumer safeguards, limits on yield-bearing stablecoins and tighter scrutiny of Trump's crypto businesses.

"In some ways, the industry doesn't really care who got elected," said Sam Stovall, chief investment strategist at CFRA Research. "They just want to know what the rules are going to be for the coming year."

Federal regulators estimate their proposed capital overhaul would cut requirements for the largest banks by roughly 4.8%, potentially freeing billions for lending and shareholder payouts. The Federal Reserve writes the rules, but Senate control shapes who fills future vacancies, and congressional committees can pressure regulators through hearings and investigations.

"It's at the regulatory level where stuff can happen that matters," Buchbinder said.

AI may be the messiest political trade of all.

Trump has made rapid data center construction an economic and national security priority. Morgan Stanley expects hyperscalers including Microsoft, Amazon, Alphabet and Meta to spend more than $1 trillion on capital projects in 2027, even as the buildout faces growing backlash over electricity bills, water use and infrastructure costs.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey from October found that 63% of registered voters oppose having a data center within 10 miles of their home, compared with just 13% who support one. The level of opposition is higher from Democrats than Republicans.

That friction has already reached Congress. In September, the House voted 417-3 to require state regulators to consider making large data centers cover the additional electricity infrastructure costs needed to serve them. The measure stalled in the Senate, 57-43 on a procedural vote, after Democrats argued it was too weak and pushed for mandatory protections instead of trusting the industry to police itself.

The midterms therefore could decide who gets the power. Democratic gains could put more grid-upgrade costs on tech developers, raising project costs just as access to power is constraining new construction.

But Congress is only part of the equation. State regulators, utilities and local governments will still decide where projects connect to the grid and which facilities get built.

"We think the election can have meaningful consequences, but on a highly localized basis," Salvatore said.

#130. Yorkshire Water boss resigns after firm downgraded

Nicola Shaw had been criticised over Yorkshire Water's pay and performance

Yorkshire Water's chief executive has announced she is to step down, just 24 hours after the company was given the lowest possible environmental rating by the Environment Agency.

Nicola Shaw, who has led the firm since 2022, will leave the role on 1 January to be replaced by Nicola Medalova, a chartered environmental health officer who previously worked for the National Grid.

In a statement on Friday, Shaw said it had been a "privilege" to lead the company but that it was the "right time" to leave her post.

Shaw has faced criticism in recent years, including for taking Â£1.9m in undisclosed payments between 2023 and 2026, while the company has come under scrutiny for its performance.

The payments were made by Yorkshire Water's parent company, Kelda Holdings, for what the firm said was "investor engagement" and "financial oversight".

On Thursday, the Environment Agency gave the firm one star out of four - its lowest possible rating - meaning Yorkshire Water was classed as a "poorly performing company".

The EA said Yorkshire Water was responsible for 260 pollution incidents in 2025, which was its highest total since 2011.

In 2024, Ofwat announced it was fining the company Â£47m for historic sewage leaks.

In her statement, Shaw said: "It has been a privilege to lead Yorkshire Water and serve the people of Yorkshire.

"Although there remains a huge amount of work to do, I am extremely proud of what our colleagues have achieved, putting the business on a stronger footing for delivery across all aspects of its work."

Yorkshire Water said Medalova, who is a former non-executive director of the company's board, would join the firm on Monday as part of a transition period before assuming the chief executive officer role full time in the new year.

She said: "I am delighted to be joining Yorkshire Water and grateful to the board for the opportunity to lead the business through its next chapter.

"Yorkshire Water faces huge challenges but also great opportunities for continued improvement and I look forward to working with Nicola Shaw, the leadership team and colleagues across the business as I get up to speed."

In December last year Shaw said "not being transparent" about receiving Â£1.3m in shareholder-funded payments across two instalments had been a "mistake", but had insisted those amounts were "not for me to decide".

She received a further Â£660,000 earlier this year. Ofwat said the payments were not performance-related.

The previously undisclosed payments had come to light the previous August when the government banned Yorkshire Water and five other water companies from paying "unfair" bonuses to bosses.

Tom Gordon, Liberal Democrat MP for Harrogate and Knaresborough, said Shaw should have stepped down "much earlier".

"Good riddance. Nicola Shaw oversaw abysmal management of our precious natural resources, trashing our rivers while receiving an eye-watering salary," he said.

"Her replacement needs to understand that residents are at their wits' end, paying ever-rising bills for polluted rivers and threats of hosepipe bans while someone at the top rakes in millions."

Meanwhile, Judith Cummins, Labour MP for Bradford South, said Yorkshire Water needed "more than a change of leadership" but a "complete change of direction".

"A new face at the top cannot wash away years of Yorkshire Water failing to put customers first," she said.

"Nicola Shaw's time as Yorkshire Water boss saw customers frustrated by poor service, skyrocketing bills, record pollution incidents, and a plummet from 'good' to the worst possible one-star environment rating.

"There must be a wholesale shift in culture that finally prioritises customers. Only then can the company begin to restore public trust."

Robbie Moore, Conservative MP for Keighley and Ilkley, said Shaw's resignation "could not have come quick enough".

He added: "I have therefore already requested an urgent meeting with the new CEO Designate Nicola Medalova to understand how she plans to improve Yorkshire Water's performance at speed.

Chris Stewart at York campaign group Ouse It Lose It said the new CEO's priority had to be looking at "failures in the system".

"There's been a continual decline in the operation of this business," he said.

Stewart said he was not surprised by Shaw's resignation but it was a "shocking indictment of the way the company has been performing, while making loud noises about how much they're putting into improving".

Naomi Tilley, campaigns manager at protest group Surfers Against Sewage, said "systematic change" was needed to fix the company.

"Regardless of who the CEO is, there's sewage entering the sea and the water companies are failing. Yorkshire Water is not an outlier," said Tilley.

"The whole system and every water company is failing in different ways and we are seeing that the system is broken and we need fundamental change.

"While these companies are set up for a profit, we're never going to see pollution curbed and stopped."

Additional reporting by David Rhodes and Grace Wood

#128. From Levi's to Primark - the fashion chains selling mended clothes

At a workshop run by Levi's, Nicholas (r) says he prefers mending not to show, while Bianca (centre) says people still see it as a sign of poverty

If Alice Brown is browsing in second-hand shops and finds a jumper with a hole, that won't stop her buying it.

"I see it as challenge," says the 27-year-old forest ranger from Lincolnshire.

She is a champion of the growing fashion for eye-catching repairs.

Over the last year she has developed her own unique style, needle-felting cute animals and insects to cover moth holes, stains and other imperfections, winning her a huge following online.

"The natural place for decoration is in the centre at the front. If the hole is under the arm or on the shoulder, it forces you to think about the design," she says.

Often dubbed "visible mending" the trend is spreading from hobby crafters and community workshops, even drawing in fast-fashion brands and reaching the designer catwalks this year.

Some mends are designed to be eye-catching

At one Saturday morning workshop in Brighton, Jayne Eagle shows her students how to attach a patch of fabric behind a tear so the cat-face design peeps through.

Then she instructs them in stitching techniques inspired by the traditional Japanese methods Boro and Sashiko. Instead of matching the thread colour to the garment, they make the mend highly decorative using geometric patterns in large white stitching.

She also offers classes in darning using contrasting vibrant colours that celebrate, rather than disguise, the repair.

Jayne Eagle sees repair as an "act of rebellion" against fast fashion

One student, Nicola, has already used her newfound skills to stitch over sunscreen marks on a T-shirt "making a feature of it" and is now delighted that people "stop seeing the stains and just see the decoration".

Another student, Lisa, says her mother's generation would never have wanted the mends to show. "Now part of it is being proud of what you've done," she says.

Lisa bought a second-hand jacket online, but snipped the fabric with the scissors when she was opening the package. She managed to patch it in a way that "looks intentional".

"I was actually pointing it out to people," she laughs.

Lisa says she's so proud of her mends she wants people to notice them

In the past mending was primarily about saving money, by wearing clothes for longer, rather than buying new. But visible mending is about celebrating that choice.

The trend took off about six years ago, Jayne says, and has boomed since.

An era of "throwaway" fast fashion from the 1980s onwards undermined the tradition of looking after and mending your clothes and people lost the skills, she says.

The revival of interest feels to her like a backlash against overproduction in the fashion industry and the damage it causes: the pollution, carbon emissions, water use, and poor labour conditions.

Alice Brown uses felt animals to cover holes and stains on her clothes

So perhaps it is surprising just how popular mended style is proving among retailers, with brands from Ralph Lauren to ultra-fast-fashion firm Shein currently selling stitched and patched garments, albeit in both of those cases brand new off the peg.

Other High Street chains are adopting a more authentic approach, offering in-house repairs and workshops.

"We want to give customers practical ways to extend the life of the clothes they already love, rather than simply replacing them," says Uniqlo's head of sustainability, Maria Ledous.

Primark, often seen as a fast-fashion retailer due to its lower prices, has also joined the trend.

"We don't think that affordability and durability should be opposites," says Vicki Swain, the brand's longevity lead.

Outdoor brand Finisterre says around a fifth of the mends it provides for customers are designed to be eye-catching, while premium artisan clothing store Toast says around a third of customers go for that option.

Part of the "magic" is that customers can bring in a garment and "choose what they'd like it to become next", says Toast chief executive Suzie de Rohan Willner.

Toast's repair services are free, but off-the-peg "renewed" pieces fetch higher prices than the originals to reflect their unique character.

Uniqlo offers decorative mending using Sashiko techniques, in keeping with the brand's Japanese heritage

Levi's, which boasts probably the longest tradition of being fixed up by its fans using anything from rhinestones to fringed cuffs, is rolling out a workshop programme this year.

The firm says its research shows younger customers in particular want to repair more but don't know how.

For now, Levi's' workshops focus on traditional mending skills, such as sewing on a button and hemming, rather than visible mends, and at a recent launch event in London, that seemed to be what its Gen Z participants were looking for.

"I'm from East London. If you have holes in your clothes people are like 'Ha ha! You're poor!'," says 16-year-old school student Bianca.

Theo, 19, says he'd rather learn how to mend without it showing.

"I would want [the jeans] to look how I originally bought them," he says.

He has a side hustle selling clothes at car boot sales and thinks traditional repair skills would make it more lucrative.

His friend Nicholas, also 19, says most people want to look "fresh".

"No-one wants to look like they came out of a dumpster," he says.

Still, Paul Dillinger, Levi's head of global design innovation, thinks more creative repairs will win them over as they pick up more skills.

"No-one has time for that perfect 'straight from the shop' thing. That's not cool.

"People crave agency," he says. "Mending is a demonstration of humanity: it's not a mustard stain, it's a memory of a great day in the ballpark."

#126. Crypto giant Binance attempting to recover $6.3bn stolen in hack linked to North Korea

Binance, the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange, says it has frozen around half of the funds stolen in a "complex" hack, after $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) was accessed in what is believed to be a record heist.

The firm said $3bn of its stolen digital assets had been secured - but many were still in the hands of unidentified hackers. It initially thought an engineer's laptop had been compromised, allowing unauthorised withdrawals, but later said the attack was wider.

Several analytics firms have linked the hack, which eclipses a $1.5bn (Â£1.12bn) heist on the Bybit exchange in 2025, to a North Korean hacking unit.

The US Secret Service said it was "working around the clock" "to identify, freeze, trace, and recover" stolen funds.

America's lead federal agency for investigating financial crimes added that it would "identify the cyber criminals responsible and bring them to justice", while Binance also said it was working with the FBI.

While Binance said its US-facing exchange was unaffected, the US would still have jurisdiction to investigate the hack and help track down stolen funds - an effort which generally involves a global law enforcement effort.

The hack also involved some US-linked assets, such as stablecoins Tether and Circle â cryptocurrencies pegged to the dollar.

Binance told its users "your money is safe, and we're covering every loss".

It had initially suspended all deposits and withdrawals - but then said in an update that "deposits remain unaffected".

Who could be behind the Binance hack and what we know so far

Binance's co-CEO Richard Teng said: "This was not carried out by one person. Our initial investigation suggested that the security incident may have involved a compromised laptop, however our subsequent investigation has uncovered additional points of breach."

Teng also describes how the hackers used "a self-destruct mechanism that destroyed tooling and traces as they went, making it more difficult for us to trace and investigate".

Initially, the company had indicated the breach "may have started with a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop" but later statements have pointed to a broader breach.

In a later update, the firm said it had "set aside" more than $6.34B in company funds to cover the stolen assets and was "actively buying" a range of crypto tokens.

The Binance hack spooked cryptocurrency markets on Friday. The price of Bitcoin was down 5% to $76,800 in Friday afternoon trading in New York.

Cryptocurrency exchange Coinbase was down 12% as investors also reacted to earnings results in which the firm reported revenue slightly below expectations. Robinhood, a financial services company that trades in cryptocurrency, was down 7.8%.

Binance, which was founded in China in 2017, allows users to trade digital assets and is estimated to have more than 300 million registered users worldwide.

The company said in 2024 that it had more than $160bn in assets under its custody.

A lot of the fear right now from Binance users - and the crypto industry at large - is whether or not Binance will actually be capable of covering this enormous financial loss.

The company has said repeatedly that customer funds will be covered and that the firm is strong enough to weather the storm.

But crypto history watchers have been pointing out online that this is exactly the kind of message that FTX CEO Sam Bankman-Fried gave before his crypto exchange collapsed in 2022, leading to huge losses for customers and the industry.

Several experts have warned customers not to regard Binance's promise to cover losses as legally binding and said they may need to pursue litigation to recover them.

Daniel Tunkel, of the London-based Edwin Coe law firm, said that would "come down to what [customers] have been promised in their contracts", and "where Binance can be sued for non-performance, if it comes to it".

Binance has been subject of regulatory scrutiny around the world.

Meanwhile, AI-powered investigations company TRM Labs said that several elements of the attack resembled earlier North Korean-linked incidents, including the Bybit hack.

In the last five years the crypto industry has been beset by enormous losses from hackers.

In nearly all cases investigators have blamed hackers from North Korea, pointing to the infamous Lazarus Group - also know as TraderTraitor. The names refer to clusters of cyber criminals accused of working directly for the regime.

Chainalysis, a crypto analytics firm, said it had "high confidence" that a North Korean is linked to the hack.

North Korea is an impoverished country with very little industry and heavy international sanctions and experts say the regime uses cyber crime as a way to bring money in.

North Korea has always denied this, but the wealth of evidence from cryptocurrency flow analysis and insider interviews from defectors is compelling.

It makes North Korea likely to be the only nation that uses hacking as a way to raise public funds.

#124. Crude oil dives to session low on Iran peace talk hopes ahead of midterms

Crude oil prices fell sharply Monday after U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said President Donald Trump prefers not to use military force against Iran and would like to resolve the conflict diplomatically.

Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, fell 6.07% to $106.54 a barrel as of 8:55 a.m. ET. U.S. West Texas Intermediate declined 6.2% to $97.65 per barrel.

Trump has previously said that he will not attack Iran before the U.S. midterms. But the oil market had priced in the risk of a major military escalation after the elections.

The president "hasn't made a decision," Rubio told reporters over the weekend.

"What the president expresses to you is incredible confidence that if we needed to do something militarily, we would have the ability to do it, and we really we have everything we need to be successful," the secretary of state said. "But we would prefer to do it peacefully."

Iranian authorities also signaled that a deal is still possible, following two days of talks mediated by Qatar, as long as demands are implemented tangibly, according to Al Jazeera.

Iranian Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei on Saturday called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

Concerns over the Middle East and Ukraine conflicts will likely linger in the near term.

"Attacks on ships and refineries in the Middle East and on civilians in Ukraine continue, doing little to inspire confidence in the prospects for lasting ceasefires in the foreseeable future," said Tamas Varga, analyst at PVM Oil Associates.

"Nonetheless, hope springs eternal," Varga added.

—CNBC's Lim Hui Jie contributed to this report.

#123. How Poundland grew from a market stall to a high street giant

Poundland has been a recognisable presence on high streets for more than 35 years

"When I told people what I wanted to do, open shops on the high street selling things for one pound, they said, 'how will you find products to sell for a pound, what about inflation, it will never work.'"

That is how Steve Smith, the founder of Poundland, describes the genesis of the budget giant - which went on to employ thousands of people and see over a million customers a week - on his own website, external.

But now the West Midlands-based retailer is teetering on the edge, after its US-based owners Gordon Brothers cut off a vital funding supply and rejected a takeover offer from Irish firm Martin Property Group.

Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators

Burnham 'ready to support' Poundland staff as retailer faces collapse

The curious case of why Poundland is struggling during a cost-of-living crisis

Having spent time on his parents stall since the age of two Wolverhampton-born businessman Smith started out in the very same place - selling items for Â£1 from his own stall.

After a Â£50,000 loan from his parents he opened the first Poundland shop, in The Octagon Shopping Centre in Burton-upon-Trent, Staffordshire, on 13 December 1990.

That first day, with a range of 624 products, the store took more than Â£13,000 - "and Poundland was born," Smith said.

Smith and his wife, Tracy, now based in Shropshire, owned the company for 16 years.

In 2006, he decided the time had come and he sold his final shares in the business for Â£250m, but later said the sale felt it was "as though somebody had died".

Steven Smith founded Poundland in 1990 with the help of his father, Kevin Smith

But by 2025, the Walsall-based company was itself sold for just Â£1.

Its owner, Polish firm Pepco, confirmed it had sold the brand for a "nominal" sum to US investment firm Gordon Brothers.

At the time Poundland had more than 800 stores across the UK and Ireland and employed around 16,000 staff.

"I did pick up the phone when I heard it was for sale but I was a bit too late," Smith said after the sale.

Now, the retailer's bosses have filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators, which will give the business 10 days to find a rescue deal.

A protest was held outside the Poundland HQ on Thursday, Claire Sutcliffe - who has been at the company for nearly a decade - among those demonstrating.

Sutcliffe said she was "very sad" to hear the news

Questioned about how she felt, the 49-year-old replied she was "very sad", adding: "Nine years is a lot working for Poundland and they've been really good to me as a Poundland family, really supportive."

Sutcliffe added that some had worked for the business for 20 years and "it's just very sad for us all".

Asked what her message was to owners Gordon Brothers, she replied: "Please just sell.

"Save our jobs, we've all got families to feed and it's... near Christmas."

About 25 people took part in a protest at Poundland in Walsall on Thursday

Professor Sarah Montano, a retail expert from the University of Birmingham, said: "The real problem for Poundland is, when they first started it was really great because everything was Â£1, that was a really unique selling point for them.

"[Today] there's competitors which are offering very similar prices and for quite a lot of products they're offering them cheaper."

She added that some competitors had "really got their finger on the key trends".

In a statement, the board of Poundland Limited said it had been "working on a formal sale process for Poundland" and despite "enormous efforts it has not been possible to identify an acceptable offer for the business as a whole".

"The process has however attracted several established retail operators who have made formal offers, collectively these cover a significant part of the business, and we believe offer the best available prospects for shareholders, creditors and to preserve jobs," they said.

"We recognise any sale process creates uncertainty and particularly when set against a challenging UK economic environment."

They also thanked "staff, suppliers and customers for their continued support".

#120. What we know about Binance's $6.3bn hack

Binance, the world's biggest crypto exchange, has been hit by a $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) hack - thought to be the biggest in history.

Half of the assets have been frozen but many remain in the hands of the hackers, the cryptocurrency giant said. It later added that it has "begun recovering" some funds.

Two analytics firms have attributed the incident to a North Korean hacking unit, though no one has claimed responsibility. Binance did not directly respond to questions about the incident's potential links to North Korea.

BBC cyber correspondent Joe Tidy explains more about the hack and what to do if you are affected.

#118. Crypto firm Binance covers 90% of losses after $6.3bn hack

Binance, the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange, says it has covered 90% of the $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) assets stolen in a hack on Thursday.

The remaining 10% may take longer to source in the required amounts, the company added.

Binance has consistently pledged to cover 100% of the losses from what is suspected to be crypto's biggest hack on record and said it has set aside more than $6.34bn in company funds to do so.

"Binance will absorb the financial impact of these stolen funds, not our users," the company said in a statement on Saturday.

In an update on Sunday, Binance said withdrawals had been restored on several of its networks, after they were suspended following the hack.

It previously said withdrawals would be resumed in phases.

The Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) said on Saturday that North Korea was responsible for the attack.

The FBI said it had observed hackers converting stolen assets into a type of cryptocurrency called Ethereum and that it is expected that the assets will then be converted again using decentralised finance and instant swap services.

Two analytics firms previously told the BBC they had robust intelligence pointing to North Korean state-backed hacking unit the Lazarus Group, also known as TraderTraitor - which was specifically named by the FBI - as the likely culprits.

Speaking to reporters on Saturday night, US President Donald Trump said, external the US is still unsure who the culprits are.

"We're learning about it. We don't know if it's North Korea - but if it is, we'll talk to North Korea about it," he said.

Binance said it had replenished the stolen assets using various funds and its own corporate treasury.

All holdings of the cryptocurrencies bitcoin, Ethereum and Tether are covered, along with seven other widely-traded crypto assets, the company said.

From Bitcoin to Binance: Crypto terms and what they mean

The hack is believed to be the largest in history, eclipsing the record of a $1.5bn heist on the Bybit exchange in 2025 - for which the FBI also said North Korea was responsible.

North Korea has always denied involvement in these attacks.

Binance's initial investigation suggested an engineer's laptop was compromised, which allowed access to unauthorised withdrawals, but a later update said they had uncovered "additional points of breach".

Richard Teng, Binance's co-chief executive officer, said the hack was carried out by more than one person, and the hackers used sophisticated methods to hide their tracks, such as a "self-destruct mechanism".

On Friday, the company said it had frozen around $3bn of stolen assets after working with law enforcement, intelligence firms and partners.

The investigation is ongoing, it added. The US Secret Service said on Friday it is "working around the clock" with private sector partners "to identify, freeze, trace, and recover funds" stolen from Binance.

Binance, founded in China in 2017, lets users buy, sell and trade digital assets - it is estimated to have hundreds of millions of users worldwide.

Crypto is a digital asset, which doesn't exist physically and is instead accessed via digital wallets or computers.

#116. City of London chief tops UK Black Powerlist

Ian Thomas, chief executive of the City of London Corporation, has been named the UK's most influential Black Briton in this year's Black Powerlist awards.

The 57-year old is the first Black person to oversee the Square Mile's governing body in its more than 900-year history.

The City of London Corporation is an administrative body with responsibility for London's central financial district, headed by its own Lord Mayor.

Thomas tops the 2027 Black Powerlist, which recognises the most powerful people of African and Caribbean heritage in the UK.

The award body said he was "a respected public-service leader with over 30 years' experience in local government".

Other influential names in this year's top ten include entrepreneurs Emma Grede and Steven Bartlett, as well as actor and filmmaker Sir Idris Elba.

Comedian Sir Lenny Henry has also been recognised, with a Lifetime Achievement award, for co-founding Comic Relief, which has raised over Â£1.6bn since 1985.

Now in its 21st year, the Black Powerlist celebrates people who have broken through barriers to reach leadership roles, thereby inspiring future generations.

"It exists to show young people of African and Caribbean heritage what is possible, in fields they might never have considered," said Michael Eboda, the list's publisher.

Sir Lenny Henry is being honoured with a Lifetime Achievement Award

Thomas said he secured the role at the head of the City of London Corporation in 2022 only following seven interviews and an election.

'When I started my journey in public service, I never, ever thought, [not] in a million years, that I'd be recognised in this way," he told the BBC.

As chief executive, he oversees around 5,000 staff with responsibilities including the City of London Police, 11,000 acres of open spaces such as Epping Forest and Hampstead Heath, and advocacy for the UK's financial and professional services sector.

Thomas was born in Sheffield to parents who made the city their home after leaving Jamaica as part of the Windrush generation.

"They worked incredibly hard and instilled that sense of discipline and respect, and that's the core of how I do business," he said of his upbringing.

Growing up, Thomas aspired to be a footballer and took part in trials alongside Alan Shearer.

Reflecting on his own change of direction, Thomas joked it was about "following dreams and if they don't come trueâ¦ there's always something else".

Before joining the City of London Corporation, Thomas served as chief executive of the Royal Borough of Kingston upon Thames where he oversaw public services and a Â£1.2bn regeneration programme.

He was appointed a CBE in the 2019 New Year Honours in the wake of the Rotherham Abuse scandal for transforming children's services during his time as strategic director for children and young people at Rotherham Metropolitan Borough Council.

Thomas replaces last year's incumbent, Afua Kyei, the Bank of England's chief financial officer and executive director, and is joined on this year's list by Comfort Ero, the President and chief executive of the International Crisis Group, who takes second place.

#115. Automakers like Rivian and Ford are ‘democratizing 3D printing’ to reduce costs and increase speed

DETROIT — Automakers including Ford Motor and Rivian Automotive are leaning into a new type of manufacturing line: 3D printing.

Manufacturers say it's helping them reduce costs and increase speed. The technology has been around for decades, but it's becoming more commoditized in the automotive industry, with 3D printers used to create everything from vehicle parts and accessories to tools for assembly plants and testing units for research and development labs.

"At this point, we don't have a choice but to scale up and begin using it through all factions of Rivian," Jonathan Dankenbring, Rivian senior manager of prototype manufacturing, told CNBC during a tour of the automaker's expansive 3D-printing operations in suburban Detroit. "We're going to be democratizing 3D printing for the entire company."

Also known as additive manufacturing, 3D printing can create three-dimensional objects from digital designs by dispensing material layer by layer, much like a traditional printer does with ink on paper. Instead of ink, the machines use a wide variety of materials such as plastics, composites, ceramics and even metals.

Printers can range from hundreds of dollars for hobby-like devices to hundreds of thousands of dollars for specialty equipment, depending on how big the product being produced is and the material it needs. They can produce individual pieces or hundreds of smaller parts in a batch using powders and resins.

Rivian said it has spent more than $5 million on 3D-printing machines, with the cost of materials for the operations growing to about $1.7 million annually.

Dankenbring said despite the costs, the machines typically pay for themselves within a year. He said the technology helps lower supplier tooling costs, streamline logistics expenses and cut production times for parts from weeks to days, if not hours.

"It's well in the millions [for savings], but the main thing is speed for Rivian," Dankenbring said.

The all-electric vehicle company's latest expansion of 3D printing includes adding new machines that can build flexible silicone parts as well as installing a bay of machines at its plant in Normal, Illinois, for employees to print parts on demand.

"3D prints should be readily available for every engineer so that they can make a part and get it in their hands as soon as possible," Dankenbring said.

Rivian has also used the technology for electric-motor models for fluid testing, creating screw guides for tooling and stamping press inserts and some production parts, including trim pieces to fill gaps in some early builds.

There's a similar push underway at larger, more-established automakers like Ford and its crosstown rival General Motors, which have used 3D printing for many years, including in their massive assembly plants.

Instead of having a bay of 3D-printing machines like at Rivian, Ford has departments for additive manufacturing at many of its plants to produce and distribute needed parts. That includes a new vending machine for 3D-printed parts for production of F-150 pickup trucks near its Dearborn, Michigan, headquarters.

"By putting the vending machine up, all the team leaders have access," said Gregory Foresi, a Ford mechanical engineer who ran Ford's 3D-printing lab at the plant from 2024 until last month. "We're able to keep that thing stocked rather than have a bunch of parts laying around in the lab. So that's been pretty huge."

Foresi used roughly two dozen machines to print off thousands of tools, some of which have been designed by employees, such as torque bit sockets, hood jackers and hand tools for smoothing adhesive.

Employees can use an app to request that specific auto parts or tools be printed. Ford's 3D-printing teams help develop them or determine what to produce next, as the machining time to build different parts can range from hours to days, if not weeks.

Foresi said creating the tools with 3D printing has allowed the automaker to uniquely design them when needed, get them faster and, in many cases, receive them at a lower cost.

Ford also has used 3D-printed parts to help keep production running. For example, Foresi said a large machining wheel was needed to maintain assembly line production and the company was able to 3D print the part until a permanent replacement from a supplier was available.

"They didn't perform as well, and it's not really a replacement for the ones that we were purchasing, but they kept the plant running," Foresi said.

Ford said it expects to have 3D labs deployed at more than 23 plants by the end of this year. Its current 3D-printing program has more than 200 machines, and there are additional printers outside the official program, the company said.

GM has used 3D-printed parts for some of its low-volume products in addition to research and development and inside plants, including recently expanding its use of industrial Stratasys printers that can cost hundreds of thousands of dollars based on use case, training and certification.

The Detroit automaker uses 135 components that are 3D-printed for its bespoke Cadillac Celestiq as well as other components for its performance Cadillac Blackwing sedans, including a unique medallion on the manual shifter knob.

Fadi Abro, senior global director of automotive and mobility at 3D-printing company Stratasys, said GM has been a great example of how companies can "standardize and then scale" 3D printing.

"The material properties improved so significantly that the use cases improved," he said, noting the company works with virtually every major automaker. "From a product perspective, we have to get the technology to become faster and more accessible, in an economic sense."

Correction: This story has been updated to reflect that Jonathan Dankenbring is Rivian's senior manager of prototype manufacturing. A previous version of this article misidentified him.

#113. US tech stocks see sharp falls as AI spending nears $1tn

Tech stocks tumbled for the second day in a row as concerns rise over the hundreds of billions of dollars firms are investing in artificial intelligence (AI).

Shares in Intel plummeted by 28% on Friday, while Google owner Alphabet saw its stock price continue to fall another 1.4% after a 12% plunge on Thursday.

Other tech stocks, including Reddit and Amazon also fell, while the Nasdaq index of the largest tech firms listed in the US, ended October with its worst monthly slide since March 2025.

While tech firms are spending big on AI, their earnings are not always meeting analysts' expectations, spooking investors and creating fears of an AI bubble.

Major tech companies have ramped up artificial intelligence spending, with Meta, Alphabet, Oracle, Amazon, and Microsoft committing to spending $800bn between them in 2026.

Alphabet's stock market value fell sharply on Thursday, taking more than $500bn (Â£374bn) off its market value after it revealed the AI summaries it now provides as the top answer to search queries were affecting its core search advertising revenue.

At the same time, Alphabet expects to spend up to $205bn this year on investments, almost entirely related to AI.

The bigger losses on Friday were elsewhere however, with Intel shedding more than $130bn in value.

The chip maker said it planned to increase spending on AI despite weaker demand for computers, a major source of demand for its micro-processors.

On Thursday, Intel's finance chief David Zinsner said the firm did not expect to bring in more cash than it spent this year and forecast lower-than-expected revenue for its fourth quarter.

"Looking ahead, we see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year," he said in the chip maker's third quarter earnings report.

In the earnings call, he said the firm had significantly ramped up investment in equipment in the third quarter and expected that to be "even more significant" in the following three months.

Amazon on Thursday revealed it had also spent more cash than it was making, and that spending had increased six-fold over the span of three months.

The company has committed to spending $220bn this year, putting the money towards investments such as AI data centres, network equipment, and servers.

Amazon shares recovered some overnight losses, but were still down by 1.4% at the end of Friday trading.

Reddit stock plunged by 29%, after the social media platform revealed weaker-than-expected daily user figures and warned that Google's growing AI-driven search was hurting referral traffic.

Stock in online game platform Roblox also fell, down by 18%, as its daily active user numbers dropped.

Several major tech firms have reported earnings this week, including Microsoft, and analysts said there were questions about the payout from the huge investments in AI.

Chris Low, from investment management firm FHN Financial, said the bulk of what was happening in the stock market was in reaction to some of the biggest tech players such as Amazon and Google.

He told Reuters the companies were "showing some pretty significant growing pains as they aggressively pursue the AI build-out".

"The thinking is that these signs suggest that the entire sector is going to slow down."

Many analysts have been debating the possibility of an AI bubble in the stock market.

The worry is that some tech firms will not be able to justify their sky-high valuations because the cost of building AI data centres and developing the software will end up being greater than the money they can make from it.

Kathleen Brookes, research director at trading firm XTB, said there have been many ups and downs in AI trade in recent months.

"Investors have turned ruthless, punishing any signs of disappointment in [third quarter] earnings," she said.

"The AI trade has had a good run in recent years, but now investors want payback, and companies like Alphabet and Intel are in the crosshairs."

However, stock market values for many of these companies remain well above where they were a year ago.

While the Nasdaq closed 2.8% lower, it is up 7.8% over the last year. Similarly Amazon is up nearly 8% over the past 12 months, while Alphabet is down 1.3%.

Brookes noted that November is typically a strong month for stocks.

"If we see AI stocks continue to struggle in the coming days, it could suggest the AI trade is past its peak, and investors will be looking for a new theme to drive markets," she said.

#111. Why North Korea's 'elite' hackers are the prime suspect in major Binance hack

Two major blockchain analytics firms say they have robust intelligence pointing to a North Korean state-backed group as the likely culprits in the $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) hack of Binance, the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange.

The Lazarus Group, a North Korean hacking unit also known as TraderTraitor, was identified "with high confidence" by TRM Labs, as well as Chainalysis.

This incident is just the latest linked to the country, one of the poorest in the world and where access to the internet is severely restricted.

Experts accuse the government of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un of relying on cybercrime to finance itself, something Pyongyang has long denied.

Though not financial, the nation's most spectacular suspected hack took place in 2014. The target: Sony Pictures, which had made the Hollywood film The Interview. The film involves a plot to assassinate Kim.

The resulting hack exposed thousands of embarrassing emails exchanged among the studio's top executives.

Who could be behind the Binance hack and what we know so far

Crypto giant Binance attempting to recover $6.3bn stolen in hack linked to North Korea

Last year, the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) said North Korea was responsible for the theft of $1.5bn on the Bybit exchange.

In the aftermath of that incident, the agency said TraderTraitor had moved to quickly launder and disperse the stolen assets, converting them to Bitcoin and other digital assets with a goal of eventually turning them into hard currency.

The FBI has broadly accused North Korea of "illicit cybercrime and virtual asset theft activities".

The nation has systematically developed its cyber operations, recruiting the most promising students from universities and channeling them into hacker groups overseen by North Korea's intelligence agency, according to reports.

Some of the cyberwarfare conducted takes place just over the nation's border in China, according to analysis from tech groups.

Watch: What we know about Binance's $6.3bn hack and what to do if you're affected

Much of its hacking activity has centred on monetary gain.

North Korean hackers are also suspected in the theft of $620m worth of cryptocurrency in 2022.

In this most recent heist, North Korean agents are accused of using a special authorisation to turn a Binance hot wallet, which is connected to the internet, into one that moved funds automatically to the attacker.

TRM said it's likely the attacker gained access to Binance's hot wallet signing system as part of the scheme.

Binance has not directly responded to the BBC's queries about the incident's potential links to the area.

The company had initially said it thought an engineer's laptop had been compromised, allowing unauthorised withdrawals, but later said the attack was wider.

"Our subsequent investigation has uncovered additional points of breach," Binance's co-CEO Richard Teng said.

Some 80% of stolen assets have been secured, a Binance spokesperson said on Friday, but the rest remains unaccounted for.

If the accusations are true, the record-making heist shows how powerful suspected North Korean hackers have become â a sign that the tools of the hacking trade have grown sharper.

Experts say the rise of artificial intelligence has also given threat actors new ways to carry out crime, through automation and other means.

In this case, it's plausible that AI was used to infiltrate Binance's systems.

As Chainalysis put it, the attacker used laundering patterns "we have not seen before".

#109. No more washing up: How Airbnb's new house rules have divided hosts and guests

Airbnb host Lisa says her Welsh holiday let was left 'a complete mess' by a group of guests

When Lisa Clewes returned to her Welsh holiday let after a group of guests checked out, she was horrified by the mess.

Just days earlier, she had met the group in person and helped with their luggage.

But after a dinner party, the house was left "in a complete mess" with broken glasses, drinks cans on the floor, pans full of food on the table and fish guts left in the garden.

"It was really unbelievable. Had I had another booking the same day, I would have had to cancel or delay check-in," says Clewes.

She charges a cleaning fee that covers around 75% of the cost of a cleaner and asks guests to empty the fridge, put rubbish in the bins and load the dishwasher.

Clewes says it typically takes cleaners four hours to turn around her property and fears that guests leaving more mess could mean increasing costs

But Airbnb's new rules mean hosts can no longer ask guests to do jobs normally carried out by cleaners, including washing and putting away dishes, stripping beds and taking rubbish off site.

The updated guidelines, which apply to bookings made globally from 20 November, state that hosts can now set seven standard house rules and add other "reasonable" requests.

Clewes says it typically takes cleaners four hours to turn around her three-bedroom property. She fears guests leaving more mess could mean hiring extra cleaners and increasing costs â expenses that may ultimately be passed on to her guests.

The changes have come as a relief to guests like Jessica Newhook, who says she has been faced with lengthy checkout lists, often on top of hefty cleaning fees.

She booked an Airbnb in Florida for a family reunion of 11 guests and says they had to "leave the property move-in ready for the cleaners", despite paying a $250 (Â£187) cleaning fee.

Newhook says the host told them to wash and fold towels, clean the bedding and kitchen, and sweep and mop the floors. She was also warned by the host that their behaviour needed to improve or they could be told to leave, because they had left chairs blocking bins.

"We had rules about everything and it was not relaxing at all."

While Newhook likes the home comforts of staying in an Airbnb, she says the combination of relentless rules and rising cleaning fees has taken away much of its appeal.

"It is getting to the point where a hotel is cheaper and I know what to expect."

Krissi Foskett, 46, from Glasgow, runs a national cleaning company and regularly stays in Airbnb properties so "can see the issue from both sides".

Krissi says there's a difference between being a respectful guest and being expected to help run the business you've just paid to stay in

During a recent stay in Stafford, she paid a Â£35 cleaning fee but was still asked to strip the beds, put on a wash, empty bins and carry rubbish down a long track to a recycling area.

"I don't particularly mind doing those things, but it did make me question what exactly I was paying the additional cleaning fee for," she says.

For Foskett, there is a difference between being a considerate guest â "putting your dirty dishes in the dishwasher" â and being "expected to prepare the property for the next guests".

Through her cleaning business, she has also seen the other extreme, with teams arriving to find broken furniture, vomit and pet urine.

While she sympathises with hosts facing tight turnarounds and rising cleaning costs, these should not be passed on to guests, she says.

"If you're running an Airbnb as a business, professional cleaning is a cost of doing business."

Anthony Marsh, who co-owns a cabin retreat in North Yorkshire with his husband, used to list five of their 12 properties on Airbnb, but says the new rules have prompted him to reduce this number to just two.

His concern with the changes is that "Airbnb has effectively given guests permission to come and leave properties a tip, and a host can't do anything about it".

Anthony and his husband used to list five of their 12 cabins in North Yorkshire on Airbnb...

... but will now list just two in light of the new rules

He believes asking guests to wash their pots and put out the bins is hardly unreasonable and, with a small cleaning team and tight turnaround times, he worries extra mess could leave properties unready for the next arrivals.

"It's common sense and common decency," he says.

His company does not charge a cleaning fee, and Marsh says most guests are respectful. But fees are more common than not. Some 63.2% of Airbnb listings charge a cleaning fee, with an average cost of Â£55 according to data from AirROI, external.

Laura Lewis runs a directory that helps holiday-let owners market their properties and connect with potential guests.

Many of her clients also list on Airbnb and she says most are unhappy with the new rules, arguing that asking guests to wash up is a matter of basic respect.

"Some people say it's factored into the cleaning fee, but I wouldn't leave a holiday let with all the washing-up to do. It's not the right thing," she says.

Laura Lewis says it's a matter of "basic respect" to wash dishes and take the bins out

The announcement has added to growing frustration among owners following increases in Airbnb's commission rates last year. Many are investing in their own websites and social media to attract direct bookings and avoid platform fees.

Lewis has taken the same approach with the three treehouses on her farm â most of her bookings are already direct and she now plans to remove her listings from Airbnb altogether.

Not all hosts are concerned about the changes, however. Matt Porsz, who hosts and manages other Airbnb properties full-time, believes the update will improve the guest experience without changing how most people behave.

Guests at his property in south-west London are asked only to lock up and return the keys. "People sometimes strip the beds or do things without me asking, and that's nice for my cleaners, but I don't expect it."

Porsz doubts the changes will lead to guests leaving properties in a worse state, pointing to Airbnb's mutual review system.

"If I put a big chore list, I wouldn't expect a five-star review and vice versa â if a guest leaves the place in a state, you're not going to give them a good review."

He believes the system encourages both sides to behave reasonably, and that most guests will continue to treat properties with respect.

"Airbnb is trying to cut out the silly stuff because it's not a good guest experience."

#107. Virgin Media O2 set to cut thousands of jobs to reduce costs

Thousands of Virgin Media O2 staff are facing redundancy as the telecoms firm attempts to save money in the face of billions of pounds of debt and tougher competition.

The broadband provider has not finalised the number of roles to be cut but it is likely be a sizeable chunk of its 16,000-strong workforce, the BBC understands.

Virgin Media O2 has billions in debt on its balance sheet and is tackling both smaller providers as well as giants like BT, which has agreed a deal to acquire rival TalkTalk, which Virgin Media O2 has firmly opposed.

The company declined to comment. Prospect, a union which represents Virgin Media O2 staff, said the cuts were "disappointing and very unsettling".

Noel McClean. deputy general secretary at Prospect, said the union was "committed to supporting our members through what is a difficult time, as well as also working with employers to explore ways to mitigate the number of redundancies and ensure retraining and redeployment of staff affected".

The majority of the affected staff are in roles that are not customer-facing and a number of senior leadership changes are already under way, the BBC has been told.

Virgin Media O2 is also expected to review its use of contractors and suppliers in conjunction with the job cuts.

As of 30 June, Virgin Media O2's total liabilities stands at Â£27.2bn while it holds assets worth Â£45.9bn, according to the firm's latest accounts.

Meanwhile, the company is dealing with fierce competition from cheaper "altnets" like Hyperoptic, Community Fibre, Gigaclear and YouFibre.

Virgin Media O2 had attempted to respond to this growing threat by buying altnet competitor Netomnia for Â£2bn via nexfibre, a joint venture owned by Virgin Media O2's owners.

However, the Competition Markets Authority (CMA) has provisionally found competition concerns in the deal, external, with a final decision to come in December, sparking anger from Virgin Media O2.

In October, Virgin Media O2 called the BT's agreed takeover of TalkTalk a "stitch-up" that had allowed its rival to "tighten its grip" over the market.

BT said its deal provided "a safety net" for TalkTalk customers as their broadband provider faced collapse without the takeover.

On Friday, BT's share price jumped 7% after the Financial Times reported Virgin Media O2 was close to agreeing a deal, external to use its rival's fibre broadband network.

The BBC has been told the deal was very close and that Virgin Media O2 was highly likely to announce it will stop all investment in fibre in its results on Tuesday.

#105. Don't get caught out by new Buy Now Pay Later rules

If you've bought something via Buy Now Pay Later (BNPL) since the summer, then you'll have been tested to see if you can afford it.

BNPL has always been a type of loan, usually paid back in instalments, but it's only been officially regulated since July.

This means stricter background checks on your ability to repay.

If you fail, then your purchase via BNPL will be blocked. If there's a chance of this happening to you, here are some ways to approach the situation.

BNPL lenders - such as Klarna, Clearpay and others - must now have regulator approval to operate, giving shoppers more access to refunds and independent rulings on complaints if things go wrong.

But new affordability checks on each BNPL transaction could also leave you blocked from services for the first time.

There are different affordability checks for different purchases, partially based on how much is borrowed.

But, with the expensive festive season approaching, here's how to avoid being blocked when time and finances are tight.

Here are four things to consider if you find your account is closed or your borrowing limit has been massively reduced, from Sara Williams, a former volunteer debt adviser at Citizens Advice who posts tips as Debt Camel on social media.

Slow down your purchases: is what you are buying essential or can you postpone it?

Be more careful about what you buy to avoid needing a high credit limit to, for example, buy lots of clothes in different sizes

Cancel subscriptions to escape the trap of having to use BNPL to make up for the cost of previous payments

Talk to debt charities for help and access to food bank vouchers if you are using BNPL for essentials

If you are struggling to make repayments on any type of loan - BNPL or otherwise - you should seek help before things spiral out of control.

The government-backed, independent, MoneyHelper website, external has a guide to finding free debt advice near you, external.

It also suggests writing down exactly what you owe, to help plan how you will repay the money.

There are also tools to work out if you are receiving all the benefits and grants you are entitled to, and a bill prioritiser, external to show which payments to make first and what to do if you miss a payment.

You should also be sceptical of supposed debt solutions you might see on your social feeds.

#102. Binance users withdraw $3.6bn after largest-ever crypto hack

Binance customers withdrew $3.6bn (Â£2.7bn) of assets from the world's biggest cryptocurrency exchange as it reopened on Sunday following what is believed to be the largest hack in history.

The firm suspended all transactions in the wake of the incident, which impacted $6.3bn worth of digital assets.

Major organisations, including the US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), have accused North Korea of being responsible for the cyber-attack.

The company said it has covered around 95% of the assets stolen in the breach but the remaining amount may take longer to source. Binance has repeatedly said it would cover all of the losses linked to the attack.

Binance said on X: "Between 06:00 and 15:30 [GMT] on November 1, we processed $4.649 billion in withdrawals and $1.01 billion in deposits, resulting in a net outflow of approximately $3.64 billion."

There was an "initial spike" in withdrawals, which later declined and "are stabilising as expected", it added.

Sunday's net withdrawals account for just over 2% of the $171bn of assets under its management, said Binance.

The firm said last week that it has reserved more than $6.3bn in funds, to deal with âthe fallout of the attack.

Binance also said withdrawals of more than two dozen major cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin and Ethereum have resumed and that more services will resume in phases once security checks are completed.

Funds linked to the Binance hack continue to be laundered, according to Alec Zebrick, senior manager in global services at Chainalysis, which has been working with the firm and governments to get the stolen money back.

"We continue to see funds being laundered quite actively," he told the BBC's Today Programme.

"The attackers are layering these funds across many thousands of addresses, across thousands of deposits," he added.

AI discovered a massive cyber-security flaw - here's what happened

The attack on Binance has been attributed to TraderTraitor, a North Korean cybercrime group also known as the Lazarus Group, which stole a then-record $1.5bn from crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

The FBI on Saturday published a list of cryptocurrency wallet addresses linked to the group and urged partners in the industry to help by blocking transactions involving them.

The exchange scrambled to urge cryptocurrency issuers to freeze affected assets.

Binance said last week that early checks suggest the incident may have started with a compromised laptop that belonged to an engineer, allowing hackers to find a way to make unauthorised transactions.

The hack hit Binance's global centralised exchange, Binance.com, while its US-facing platform has said it was not affected by the issue.

Watch: What we know about Binance's $6.3bn hack and what to do if you're affected

Binance said it had replaced customers' assets using a combination of sources, including its corporate treasury as well as its futures insurance and emergency reserve funds.

"Our commitment remains the same: Binance will fully cover the financial impact of the funds stolen in this incident," the firm said.

Cryptocurrencies are digital assets traded on exchanges such as Binance. Unlike cash, they do not exist in physical form and are managed on computers, smartphones and other digital devices.

Binance was founded in China and has hundreds of millions of registered users globally.

Why North Korea's 'elite' hackers are the prime suspect in major Binance hack

#99. Anthropic releases amended IPO prospectus with third-quarter financials

Anthropic reported updated financials as part of an amended Form S-1 filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Monday, giving investors a more comprehensive look into the state of its business.

The artificial intelligence company said it generated $33.24 billion in revenue for the nine months ended Sept. 30, up roughly 1,200% from the same period last year. It reported a $104 billion net loss during the period, after losing $25.1 billion over the same stretch in 2025.

Anthropic unveiled its highly anticipated prospectus last week, bringing the company one step closer to a potentially historic IPO. The initial filing only included the company's financials through the first half of the year.

In the amended S-1, Anthropic said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

#98. Poundland's former owner offers to invest as minister prepares to meet board

Poundland has been given until Thursday to find a rescue deal, putting up to 12,000 staff at risk.

Poundland's former owner has said he would invest in the firm as a government minister said she would meet the firm's board.

Steve Smith, who left the Walsall-based business in 2006, said he had attempted to contact owners Gordon Brothers having been "really surprised" to learn about them filing for administration when sales were up 11% on last year.

The BBC understands that Business Minister Kate Dearden will meet with senior Poundland executives later this week to discuss the company's threatened insolvency.

US-based Gordon Brothers said it was evaluating interest for the whole business and formal and indicative offers for significant viable parts of it.

Gordon Brothers, who bought the firm from Polish retail group Pepco last year for Â£1, has stopped funding the business and rejected a takeover offer from Irish firm Martin Property Group (MPG).

The retailer has given the business until Thursday to find a rescue deal, putting up to 12,000 staff at risk.

Steven Smith founded Poundland in 1990 with the help of his father, Kevin Smith

It's understood the meeting with Deardon will be predominantly a "fact finding" meeting with government sources conceding there is limited scope for the government to intervene in a commercial transaction albeit one that puts over 10,000 jobs and hundreds of stores at risk.

The meeting was first reported by Sky News.

There is no current meeting planned between the government and executives from current owners, US private equity group Gordon Brothers.

Concerns have been raised that Gordon Brothers have declined an offer from another buyer to keep Poundland afloat in order to ensure repayment of Â£30 million loan it inherited after buying the company for Â£1.

'Poundland closure would leave a void in our town'

How Poundland grew from a market stall to a high street giant

Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said the government was "ready to support" staff as he also called for answers over why an offer to buy the business had been rejected.

Smith, from Wolverhampton and who set up the firm in 1990 with a store in Burton-upon-Trent, Staffordshire, said he contacted the owners over the last three months but had no reply. He had thought that was good news as he assumed the firm must have had offers on the table.

"And maybe I was a bit too late, but I'd certainly... with talking to the staff and how Poundland's performing at the moment, I'd certainly, if there's an opportunity, I definitely want to put some money back in there."

He said he was still in touch with staff there at levels within the business and "even last week" sales were 11.2 % upon last year.

"Although Poundland made a loss last year, they're going to make a good profit this year, so it's just crazy."

Staff held a protest outside Poundland's Walsall-based HQ last week

He warned that the two or three thousand suppliers to the firm only worked for Poundland so they would be directly affected by its collapse.

"This is not just about 12, 000 jobs. I'm estimating more like 20 or 30,000 jobs," he added.

The company has 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, and Smith said it had between four and five million customers a week, so with Poundland not being present on high streets would "cause a lot of other retailers to fall as well".

Liam Byrne, chair of the Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee and Labour MP for Birmingham Hodge Hill and Solihull North, said he felt the company could be saved and "the whole deal stinks to high heaven right now".

"Because I think the issue that we've got is, you've got an owner that could well be trying to cut and run, to sell a business that is in fact a going concern in order to maximize their own profits and put in jeopardy 12,000 British jobs on the high street."

"Our message today to government and to ministers is, do not leave this to the last minute, which is Thursday... please get on the case now to get everybody around the table to maximize every option to save as much of the business as possible," he added.

In a statement, the board of Poundland Limited said it had been "working on a formal sale process for Poundland" and despite "enormous efforts it had not been possible to identify an acceptable offer for the business as a whole".

"We recognise any sale process creates uncertainty and particularly when set against a challenging UK economic environment."

Gordon Brothers said: "While Martin Property Group had been identified as the preferred bidder by Poundland's management team, attention must now turn to other active bids."

The interest, formal and indicative offers were being evaluated "with a view to minimising uncertainty for Poundland's people and all relevant stakeholders."
